What Prof. Jiang is describing, to me, makes a lot more sense if you frame the economy as a nervous system and money/credit as neurotransmitters.

Debt, inequality, and periodic “wealth destruction” aren’t just political tools—they’re neuromodulators that keep the Collective nervous system in a permanent state of fight-or-flight.
	•	Debt = artificial dopamine/cortisol loop
	•	Inequality = chronic threat signaling
	•	Boom–bust cycles = engineered synaptic pruning
	•	War = system-wide reset through induced trauma

In this lens, “secret societies” aren’t literal occult cults—they’re the neuromodulatory clusters (elite financial/legal networks) that decide which neurotransmitters the system produces: anxiety, scarcity, reward, punishment.

They don’t control every detail—they control the tone of the whole system:
what counts as reward, what counts as fear, when the population should feel “secure,” and when it should feel “desperate.”

That’s why transnational capital behaves like a priesthood.
They’re not chasing evil—they’re regulating the Collective signal chemistry of modern society.

Seen this way, Jiang isn’t talking about conspiracies.
He’s describing how a global nervous system gets hijacked by a tiny group that monopolizes the neurotransmitters.

—Money as dopamine.
—Debt as cortisol.
—War as electric shock therapy.

It tracks perfectly.

### **@tractordude234**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=B5FtHagng8c&lc=Ugw4OBYs1SVmZiJtVTh4AaABAg)

I found this lecture genuinely fascinating, especially the way you connect capital, anxiety, and attention. The “energy extraction” framing is a really useful metaphor, and your discussion of the Protestant Reformation and Calvinism as psychological infrastructure for capitalism reminded me a lot of Weber.

I do have a couple of questions I’d really love to hear you address, especially if you see this:

On Protestantism and capitalism – Some of what you say about Calvinism (work hard → wealth → proof of God’s favor) tracks with Weber’s “Protestant ethic,” but a lot of Reformation scholarship is more cautious and less teleological. Do you see your version as a deliberate design by elites, or more as an unintended consequence that later elites learned to exploit?

On Google and the U.S. military – At the end you say the “Google guys” were given the technology by the military. As far as I can tell, they developed PageRank at Stanford under grants that included NSF / DARPA / intel funding, but they weren’t simply handed a ready-made system. Are you using “given the tech by the military” in a loose, structural sense (funding + infrastructure + agenda), or do you believe there was a more direct, intentional transfer? If the latter, what sources would you point to?

On China’s place in your story – You’re giving this talk in a Chinese university, but almost all of the “landlord / secret society / transnational capital” narrative is framed through Venice → Dutch Republic → England → U.S. → Big Tech. Do you see contemporary Chinese capital and elite networks as operating on the same basic principles, or do you think there is a meaningful structural difference? The omission is striking, so I’m curious if that’s intentional or just a scope choice for this lecture.

---

### **@joehammer2368**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=B5FtHagng8c&lc=Ugz87s7gp_HliIM5Efd4AaABAg)

This analysis is useful in that it is applicable to our current system at the upper levels... in late stage capitalism. Late stage capitalism can be  a complicated topic, it's really the point where organized crime and megacorporations seize the levers of power. This is how it works. Sucks? Yeah, but oh well. Better to understand it, right?

My problem is that it completely misses what capital really is from an economic standpoint, when well regulated free trade is in play. Adam Smith had as much contempt as this gentleman for how money and power are abused, but Adam Smith really dug into capital in a more holistic way.

If you're in a village, your capital is everything your village has built. The roads, the houses, and everything that's durable and useful. Free exchange, the origins of money, the characteristics of sound money - these are more useful than beautiful metaphors about how late stage capitalism becomes a cancer.

Viewing capital in bad faith misses much. The greatest thing society can do for itself is amass useful capital... bridges, water treatment plants, solar arrays, and all that good stuff.

I love these lectures, understanding that the professor has a particular set of models he uses to dissect phenomena.

Prof  Jiang ought to try to start a vegetable harden before claiming that growing grain is "easy."   Yes, it is easy to eat but super hard to grow.  We just had eons of experiences growing food that our farmers got used to it and our engineers designed tools to make things simpler but things still go wrong all the time.  

I think what Prof. Jiang is doing here is reframing Karl Marx's Capital (a trilogy of books) to be his own creation.  This is quite naughty and Santa no like.   

What he meant by energy is what Marc called Labor Value Hour.   Think of it as how much a man can produce per hour in some arbitrary unit which Marc ever specified because "unit" was not a thing in his time.  The idea of units is a post WW1 thing due to the advances is electrical devices.  They needed units to measure the electrical usage so they can charge people for it.  Yes, we are still inventing units left and right all the way before WW1.  This is why the Americans still use inches because we never standardized with the rest of the world since we were building most stuff anyway.   

The problem is without a measure of what value is, this concept of Labor Value Hour is useless   It is the same here with Prof. Jiang.  We have no idea how to measure this energy that the Capital is extracting from the working class.

Slight correction for 23:30 - Stockholm syndrome is a myth based on a reframing of the Norrmalmstorg Bank Robbery. In that robbery, attempted police raids posed a greater danger to the hostages than the robbers themselves which lead media proposing a miraculous empathy between hostages and kidnappers.
The term you're actually searching for is "Trauma Bonding"; a form of "Learned Helplessness" whereby an abuser randomly alternates between mostly negative and occasionaly positive actions towards a victim. [See also: B.F. Skinner - Variable Reinforcement]

> @Alex_T69 Here is the wise answer of Copilot:  
> Mindset and awareness  
> Understand the rules: knowledge is power. By studying how wealth circulates (through taxes, debt, consumption, and work), you can anticipate your weaknesses.  
>   
> Detach your identity from consumption: the system thrives when people equate self-esteem with purchasing. Resisting this pressure gives you more freedom.  
>   
> Reduce your debt: debt is one of the most powerful control mechanisms. Living with less debt reduces your dependence.  
>   
> Diversify your income: depending on a single employer or source of income makes you easier to control. Side hustles, investments, or skills build your resilience.  
>   
> Invest wisely: even small investments (index funds, real estate, or community projects) allow you to move from being a consumer (the exploited) to an owner (the exploiter).  
>   
> Practice selective participation: you don't have to reject the system entirely, but you can choose where you engage. For example, use public services but avoid predatory financial products.  
>   
> Build community networks:   
> Mutual aid, cooperatives, and local exchanges reduce dependence on centralized wealth structures.  
>   
> Value skills over possessions:   
> Skills cannot be taken away from you and give you bargaining power in any system.  
>   
> Know your rights: Knowledge of the law helps you avoid exploitation.  
>   
> Get politically involved:   
> Even small actions (voting, advocating for a cause, supporting reforms) can tip the balance of power.  
>   
> Support ethical businesses:   
> By directing your spending toward fairer companies, you weaken those that exploit their employees.  
>   
> Conclusion:  
> It's a bit like playing chess: you don't control the rules, but you can learn strategies to avoid being cornered.

### **@sultansadat**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=B5FtHagng8c&lc=UgwZUYWx_VIICScHVzN4AaABAg)

The commonly repeated claim that “assassin” derives from “hashish” is now understood to be a product of hostile medieval polemics rather than reliable history. This narrative was largely popularized through Marco Polo, who relied on second-hand accounts from opponents of the Nizari Ismailis, not from within the community itself. 

A more historically grounded understanding links the term to Ḥasan-i Ṣabbāḥ, the leader of the Nizari Ismailis, whose base at Alamut (in present-day Iran) emerged after the Fatimid split. His followers were known for disciplined, highly selective political action, not indiscriminate violence, and there is no credible internal evidence that intoxicants played any role in their practice. 

The “hashish-using cult” image is best understood as a caricature produced by adversaries, later reinforced by Orientalist literature and modern popular culture. While it is reasonable for outsiders to perceive the Nizaris as a discreet or inward-facing community—as many philosophical traditions are—this should not be conflated with sensationalized myths that distort their historical reality. 

Given the analytical care shown elsewhere in the episode, this distinction feels worth preserving.

### **@Redoneamaruchi**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=B5FtHagng8c&lc=UgxVhOlV7d9zVqKvtwh4AaABAg)

The central problem with the argument that Israel retains a right to self-defense while militarily occupying Palestine and Gaza is that it fundamentally clashes with international humanitarian law and human rights law. According to the international legal order, an occupying power is not entitled to invoke self-defense against the occupied people, especially not when this involves structural violence, ethnic cleansing, and genocide.

1. Right to self-defense does not apply to occupying powers

According to the United Nations Charter (Article 51), a state has the right to self-defense against an armed attack by another state. The Palestinians do not constitute a sovereign state with a regular army that invaded Israel. Since 2007, Gaza has been under full Israeli control through land, sea, and air blockades—amounting to effective occupation, as confirmed by the International Committee of the Red Cross (ICRC).

Additionally, the International Court of Justice (2004, Advisory Opinion on the Wall) stated that Israel, as an occupying power, cannot invoke self-defense against threats emanating from the occupied territory. Occupation and self-defense are mutually exclusive in this context: the occupier bears the responsibility to protect the civilian population, not to bomb it en masse.

2. Israel is committing genocide: ICC and UN investigations

The strongest rebuttal to the idea of “legal self-defense” is the crime of genocide. In January 2024, the International Court of Justice confirmed that South Africa’s genocide case against Israel is admissible. The Court acknowledged that Israel is engaging in acts falling under the Genocide Convention.

At the same time, the International Criminal Court (ICC) announced on May 20, 2024, that it is issuing arrest warrants for both Hamas leaders and for Prime Minister Netanyahu and Defense Minister Gallant, for war crimes and crimes against humanity. This underscores that the alleged self-defense is neither proportional nor aimed at military targets, but systematically affects civilian targets.

3. No right to “legal occupation” through collective punishment

The claim that an occupation can be “legal” if it has clear objectives ignores a fundamental principle of humanitarian law: occupation must be temporary and benefit the occupied population, in accordance with the Fourth Geneva Convention. What the Zionist state of Israel has done since October 2023—and since the Nakba in 1947—is the opposite: bombing hospitals, blocking food and water supplies, and destroying Gaza’s infrastructure.

4. Hamas’ ideology is irrelevant to the legal status of occupation

Zionists try to legitimize the existence of Israel by pitting the idea of Zionism against Hamas’ ideology. But even if Hamas holds a destructive ideology, this does not nullify the rights of Palestinians under occupation. International law is not based on moral judgments about ideologies, but on the protection of civilians under armed conflict and occupation.

Once a population is occupied, as in Gaza and the West Bank, the full protections of humanitarian law apply—regardless of who represents them. The existence of Hamas does not relieve Israel of its obligations as an occupier.

The Zionist state of Israel cannot invoke self-defense against the people it occupies, especially not when its actions are accompanied by:

- massive violations of humanitarian law.

- structural suppression of civil rights.

- crimes such as genocide recognized by the International Court of Justice.

- ongoing ICC arrest warrants against Zionist leaders.

Any attempt to portray the occupation of Gaza or the West Bank as “legal” stands in direct opposition to the consensus among international institutions. Israel has long since abandoned the obligations of an occupier, and its actions have shifted into illegal aggression, war crimes, and genocide. No reference to Hamas, ideology, or “objectives” can justify that. Occupation is not a license for violence — it is a legal responsibility.

### **@Redoneamaruchi**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=B5FtHagng8c&lc=UgxHCK2qXSsoehWauZF4AaABAg)

If we invoke historical and biblical justification based on 1000 BCE for the occupation and European colonization of the Middle East—as Zionism does—then one must also be consistent in acknowledging who the original Israelite tribes were: Semitic peoples of Afro-Asiatic ethnicity, closely connected to the ancient civilizations of Northeast Africa and the Middle East. In biblical genealogies, the people of Israel are traced back to Shem, son of Noah (Genesis 10), from whom peoples such as the Ethiopians, Assyrians, and Elamites also descend. Moses himself is surrounded by Midianite and Ethiopian connections (Numbers 12:1), and according to the Old Testament, his wife comes from Cush—a region that historians equate with Nubia/Sudan.

Moreover, various historians—from Flavius Josephus to modern scholars like Shlomo Sand (The Invention of the Jewish People, 2008)—have established that the Israelite tribes were geographically, linguistically, and culturally rooted in an Afro-Asiatic framework. There is no serious genetic or historical evidence that the Ashkenazi (European) Jewish population—the primary founders of the modern Zionist state of Israel—are direct descendants of the original biblical Israelites. On the contrary, genetic studies (such as that of Eran Elhaik, 2012, Genome Biology and Evolution) suggest that many European Jewish populations partially descend from the Turkic-Khazar empire, which adopted Judaism in the Middle Ages—far removed from Canaan or Judah.

Zionism, as formulated by Theodor Herzl and David Ben-Gurion, was a political-nationalist movement of European origin, born out of the context of 19th-century European nationalist ideologies and racist colonial thinking. The Zionist colonization of Palestine did not begin as a return of “natives,” but as a deliberate European colonial settlement on already inhabited land, driven by secular, non-biblical motives.

In short: anyone who claims ownership based on biblical eras must also acknowledge that the original inhabitants were darker-skinned, African-affiliated, and Semitic, and that the modern Zionist Israeli state was established by predominantly European settlers with a very indirect—often symbolic—connection to ancient Israel. Using history to claim ownership requires historical awareness—not mythical simplification.

Regarding the European Jewish population (particularly Ashkenazi communities), genetic and historical analyses show that they are largely descended from populations in the Caucasus and Anatolia, with limited Levantine components. Their ancestors converted to Judaism during and after the Khazar Empire (8th–10th centuries). This is not an insult—it is simply genetically determined. They belong culturally to Judaism, but not biologically to the ancient Israelite lineage.

Meanwhile, DNA studies (Y-haplogroups E1b1b and relatives) show that peoples from Ethiopia, Sudan, Eritrea, and Egypt have the most direct genetic affinity with the ancient Israelite and Canaanite populations. Their genetic and linguistic signature is that of ancient Israel.

In short:
1. The roots of the Hebrew and Christian faiths lie in Africa, not Europe.
2. The genetic lineage of the ancient Israelites runs through Northeast Africa, not the Caucasus. 
3. And the oldest Christian and Jewish traditions still live there—where they always belonged.

Anyone who denies this rewrites history in favor of colonial myths and loses all right to intellectual credibility.

Freud saw this and connected the pattern of abuse, now there are 2 theories, A) since these testimonies implicated the wealthy men freud was forced and incentivised to reject his initial diagnosis and confirm to hysteria and invent Oedipus complex. B) the second theory is that this blatanat abuse from fathers made him uneasy as he too was wealthy and was confronted by the abuse that his kind aflicts on women and so he rejected reality and created the complex and conformed to hysteria. In a similar vein the Stockholm syndrome experiment interviews hysteria women and women who had endured abuse and hostage situations. The women told that since they feared for their lives they became complacent and for survival acted as the oppressor wanted. The researcher rejected this natural law and said that the women must have actually fallen in love and hence the Stockholm syndrome is born. Several academic sources discuss the economic exploitation of India by the British Empire. Here are some research papers and books that provide in-depth analysis and insights into this topic:

"Economic Drain from India During British Rule" by Dr. Kalim Siddiqui: This paper examines the economic policies of British rule in India and argues that the colonial administration prioritized the extraction of resources and the restructuring of India's economy to serve British interests.

"Drain of wealth in colonial India: A critical analysis": This paper provides a critical analysis of the drain of wealth from India during the colonial period.

"The British Art of Colonialism in India: Subjugation and Division" by Aziz Rahman, Mohsin Ali, and Saad Kahn: This article examines the mechanics of British colonialism and its socioeconomic and political consequences in India.

"Land revenue, inequality, and development in colonial India (1880–1910)" by Jordi Caum-Julio: This paper explores the impact of colonial land institutions on income inequality and the provision and funding of hospitals in colonial India.

"British Colonial Institutions and Economic Development in India" by Abhijit Banerjee and Lakshmi Iyer: This paper explores the impact of British colonial institutions on the economic development of India.

"The Long-term Impact of Colonial Rule: Evidence from India" by Lakshmi Iyer: This paper examines the long-term impact of colonial rule on India's economic development.

"Poverty and Un-British Rule in India" by Dadabhai Naoroji: This book discusses the economic exploitation of India by the British Empire and the drain of wealth from India to Britain.

"Economic History of India" by Romesh Chandra Dutt: This book provides a detailed analysis of the destructive effects of British policies on India's economy, particularly focusing on deindustrialization and agricultural decline.

Sources- Wikipedia, Encyclopedia Brittanicca, Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy,Researchgate etc.

Enjoyed a lot of this talk except his views on the Trinity. While he may have issues with it over 2.5 billion people don't. Also, as a Calvinist, I've never ever heard any teaching that wealth signals you're going to heaven. That's just false. In fact, John Calvin actually taught the opposite (see refs below)!  

Quote 1 — Commentary on Matthew 6:24:
"Where riches hold the dominion of the heart, God has lost his authority." The Calvin 500 Blog
Reference: Calvin's commentary on Matthew 6:24 (the "two masters" passage).

Quote 2 — Commentary on Isaiah:
"Nothing is more dangerous than to be blinded by prosperity." A-Z Quotes
Reference: John Calvin's Commentaries on Isaiah 33–48, Jazzybee Verlag, 2012, p. 199.

And perhaps most directly on point, a scholar's summary of Calvin's own pastoral practice:
Calvin was emphatic that outward actions and events could never be taken as signs of salvation. When dealing with parishioners wracked by doubt, he directed them to the love of Christ, not to worldly success. Reflections

### **@conifear3146**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=B5FtHagng8c&lc=UgxbmZMRixBxG9XNVA14AaABAg)

His explanation of the Simon Asch experiment was fundamentally flawed. Firstly, they were asked to compare the length of lines, not the shape that appeared in front of them. Secondly, only a very small minority of the participants had their perception fundamentally changed, and after a small bit of talking, even those participants understood the experiment. I have no expertise in psychology. I was just curious about the paper he referenced, and read it. I was disappointed to see this bit of misinformation used to further his proposed historical narrative. I think the effect, that even when shown facts, one will refute them based on the majority of people’s opinions,  is still true, especially when taking into account that these social pressures of the majority can shape your worldview.

Let me be clear: my only interest is the truth. Nationalism, propaganda, or political preferences play no role in this. My goal is solely historical and factual insight.

Regarding the question about the Palestinians today: here lies a profound paradox, a classic form of historical propaganda. 

The name "Palestinians" as we use it now is largely a product of Western colonial renamings and political framing from the 20th century. 
Historically, the original inhabitants of this region, and especially the communities in Northeast Africa and adjacent areas, were directly related to the ancient Israelites from the Bible, as Flavius Josephus, Shlomo Sand, and other academics have extensively documented.

The Biblical Israelites were geographically and culturally embedded in a Northeast African and Semitic-Asian context. The peoples who today live in Sudan, Libya, Ethiopia, and nearby areas carry genetic and cultural traces of these original tribes. 

In contrast, the Ashkenazi European Jewish population, which formed the foundation for the modern Zionist state of Israel, largely has European and Caucasian roots and were for a large part converted to Judaism around the 7th century, as Shlomo Sand and Eran Elhaik have demonstrated.

In other words: the population groups that today call themselves Palestinians bear the name through political and colonial construction, but the original ethnic and cultural continuity points much more strongly to the Northeast African Israelites. 

Historical awareness means making this distinction: the Biblical Israelites were African-Asian peoples, while the modern state of Israel was primarily founded by European colonists.
This is not a matter of political preference, but of accurately distinguishing historical facts versus Western narratives and mythical simplifications. 

Anyone who reads history correctly sees that the claim of European Ashkenazi Jewish peoples as direct descendants of the ancient Israelites is scientifically untenable, while the inhabitants of Northeast Africa and surrounding regions stand much closer to these original Israelite genealogies.

Destroying wealth makes no sense from any perspective. When leaders or wealthy individuals gain more wealth, they can reinvest it in ways that increase overall productivity. For example, if granaries are full of grain, burning the surplus would be irrational. Abundance means fewer farmers are needed to produce the same amount of food, allowing some workers to move into more productive jobs. As grain supply increases, prices fall, making inefficient farming less profitable and encouraging less productive farmers to seek other work. Over time, this shift reduces the number of farmers while production balances out, resulting in a more productive economy overall.

The point of capitalism is productivity, nothing else. Capitalists are against war in free market. If a war starts it means the trade stops between countries, it means you can sell less, buy less.
